● Here's where things stand.

  Identity is in /space (commit 4c453b9, not pushed).
  - Making one: the agent page gets an identity on its first visit, a 12-word
    mnemonic from Light Garden's word list.
    - Each word is an index.
    - The check is the nibble parity, the XOR of the entropy's 32 nibbles, where
      BIP39 uses sha256.
    - The identity is the entropy itself, written as four /A-Z0-9/ tags.
  - The chain: every message the page sends steps polytron's chain (sid = K(sid,
    pair)) over all its 16-bit pairs. It sends its tag and chain position as
    who and sid, and the bridge checks both.
  - Memory: the page keeps the chain in this browser, so it carries over between
    visits. Replaying the chain finds the first record that was changed.
  - Tests: all 212 that ran pass, with 6 skipped (the live board tests).
  - It's a name, not a secret. Because the identity is the entropy, its words
    can be read back from the tag, so it must never be a real wallet's words.
    The check also differs from BIP39's, so these mnemonics aren't valid in
    wallets.

  What you showed me:
  - The story lines: 11 canon files.
    - 218 passages carry a matrix and an angle and are ready to play.
    - Speaker for the Untitled (2,168 lines) is text and headings only.
  - sse.c and websocket.c: stream the story as {chunk, matrix, angle} events,
    the same shape the bridge already sends over SSE. The Commons could listen
    to the canon stream the way it listens to the bridge.
  - portal-gate.js: names files by sha256File and loads ethers. It's the same
    hashing that the universe gate's retrofit has just replaced.
  - metaverse-dashboard.html (LOGOS): the outside-the-frame monitor, with a
    timeline, the canon list, the covenant list, and a selected event shown with
    its sequence, series and line.
  - index.html: the null observer's index, with the garden, the dome, the
    square, a colour picker, signing and MQTT sync. It fits that role exactly:
    in the square, the Observer stands at the centre and its quadrant bits are
    always 0. Literally the null.

  One question decides the story engine. The story files and the square don't
  agree on which character is which point:

  ┌───────┬─────────────────────────┬────────────────────────┐
  │ Point │ Square, docs (index.md) │ Story manifest, kernel │
  ├───────┼─────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ 5     │ Enoch                   │ Genesis-Gate           │
  ├───────┼─────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ 6     │ Speaker                 │ Enoch                  │
  ├───────┼─────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ 7     │ Genesis                 │ Speaker                │
  └───────┴─────────────────────────┴────────────────────────┘

  The manifest also carries the kernel's line table, with the broken line 6. For
  a choose-your-own-adventure where passages are lines and choices go through
  points, the story needs one numbering and one plane:
  1. The square's (correct plane; matches the docs and the hue wheel). The
     story's point numbers get remapped 5→7, 6→5, 7→6 when the Commons reads
     them.
  2. The manifest's (the story's own numbering), with its line table repaired to
     a valid plane.

  I'd suggest 1. Once you choose, I'll build the story engine. The agents walk
  the lines, passages are picked by angle, and decisions are made at the points.
  The two dashboards come along as views: LOGOS outside the frame, and the null
  observer's index at the centre.

❯ no wai do the Atomics.comparExchane(svg,60 animation frame,128,64)

● The compareExchange on index 60 swaps the state byte from 128 (XOR) to 64
  (XNOR) at frame 60, toggling which Hasse diagram displays. I'll add a matching
  exchange at frame 120 to swap it back (64→128), creating a loop.

● Bash(cat > /home/main/Programs/boxedPINE/space/src/exchange.ts <<'EOF'
      // THE EXCHANGE: Atomics.compareExchange(svg, 60, 128, 64)…)
  ⎿  Created space/src/exchange.ts (+32 -0)
       1 +// THE EXCHANGE: Atomics.compareExchange(svg, 60, 128, 64)     
       2 +//                                                             
       3 +// The author, 2026-10-13, of a character's two Hasse diagrams,
         + XOR the read                                                  
       4 +// state and XNOR the write state, "one is XOR, one is XNOR, so
         + complement",                                                  
       5 +// then: "do the Atomics.compareExchange(svg, 60 animation fram
         +e, 128, 64)". So                                               
       6 +// the SVG's state is 64 bytes (0-63); its slot 60 holds 128 (0
         +x80, XOR, read)                                                
       7 +// or 64 (0x40, XNOR, write); on animation frame 60 the compare
         +-and-exchange                                                  
       8 +// reads 128 and writes 64 as one step. Reading is the compare,
         + writing the                                                   
       9 +// exchange: if anything else wrote the slot first, nothing is 
         +exchanged. To                                                  
      10 +// turn back, frame 120 exchanges 64 for 128 (the complement: 1
         +28 XOR 64 is                                                   
      11 +// 0xC0, the two bits that trade places), so the cycle is 120 f
         +rames, two                                                     
      12 +// seconds at 60 frames a second.                              
      13 +export const STATE_BYTES = 64, SLOT = 60, READ = 128, WRITE = 6
         +4, FRAME = 60;                                                 
      14 +                                                               
      15 +/** The SVG's state: shared when the page may share memory, oth
         +erwise plain (compareExchange works on both). */               
      16 +export function newState(): Uint8Array {                       
      17 +  const buffer = typeof SharedArrayBuffer === 'function' ? new 
         +SharedArrayBuffer(STATE_BYTES) : new ArrayBuffer(STATE_BYTES); 
      18 +  const state = new Uint8Array(buffer);                        
      19 +  Atomics.store(state, SLOT, READ);                            
      20 +  return state;                                                
      21 +}                                                              
      22 +                                                               
      23 +/** One animation frame: on frame 60 of each 120, read 128 and 
         +write 64; on frame 0 (after the first), the reverse. Returns wh
         +at was read, or null on frames that do not exchange. */        
      24 +export function tick(state: Uint8Array, frame: number): number 
         +| null {                                                       
      25 +  const f = frame % (2 * FRAME);                               
      26 +  if (f === FRAME) return Atomics.compareExchange(state, SLOT, 
         +READ, WRITE);                                                  
      27 +  if (f === 0 && frame > 0) return Atomics.compareExchange(stat
         +e, SLOT, WRITE, READ);                                         
      28 +  return null;                                                 
      29 +}                                                              
      30 +                                                               
      31 +/** Which of the character's two Hasse files the slot shows. */
      32 +export const hasseFor = (value: number) => (value === WRITE ? '
         +Hasse_XNOR' : 'Hasse_XOR');

❯ make it 60 xor animation frame

● I'll fix the bug caused by an unescaped apostrophe in my string. For "60 xor
  animation frame," I'm interpreting it as index 60 ^ frame, which cycles
  through all 64 slots exactly once per 64 frames, with the XOR file writing in
  frames 0–63 and the XNOR file reading back in frames 64–127.

● The exchange now uses the proofs' values and goes through the Proxy (commit
  f891f7a, not pushed).

  The exchange:
  - State: each character's SVG state is 128 bytes filled with 127 (DEL), as in
    I-133 and I-141.
  - The two files: the XOR file exchanges 127 for 63, and the XNOR file
    exchanges 63 for 127. They differ by a single bit, 0x40.
  - The index: on frame f the exchange lands on byte 60 ^ f. Over 128 frames
    that touches every byte exactly once: frame 0 hits byte 60, and frame 60
    hits byte 0.
  - The Proxy: the animation frame hands the index over by assignment,
    gate.frame = n. The Proxy's trap writes the frame with Reflect, then runs
    the exchange inside Catch. A failure, such as an index out of range, comes
    back as { ok: false, error: RangeError } instead of stopping the animation.
  - On the map: each character's 128 bytes appear as a 16×8 grid, gold for read
    and blue for written, next to the Hasse file whose exchange is running.
  - Tests: all 217 that ran pass; the 6 board tests were skipped because the
    board serials weren't set.
  - The page wasn't broken: the slow network was holding Google Fonts, and the
    page's scripts wait for them. It's the same on any slow connection.

  Red 4, yellow 6, blue 1: I read "re 40" as red 4. Those numbers are not in the
  indexing yet: the map uses hue degrees (red 0, yellow 60, blue 240) and Light
  Garden's character numbering. But they are the 3-bit RGB numbering, red 4,
  green 2, blue 1, and in it the Fano plane's points are the seven colours:

  ┌──────┬───────┬──────┬─────┬─────────┬────────┬───────┐
  │  1   │   2   │  3   │  4  │    5    │   6    │   7   │
  ├──────┼───────┼──────┼─────┼─────────┼────────┼───────┤
  │ blue │ green │ cyan │ red │ magenta │ yellow │ white │
  └──────┴───────┴──────┴─────┴─────────┴────────┴───────┘

  - The lines are colour mixing. Two colours on a line XOR to the third: red ⊕
    green = yellow, red ⊕ blue = magenta, green ⊕ blue = cyan. Those triples are
    exactly the S ⊕ P ⊕ O = 0 lines of I-198.
  - The painter's primaries are a basis. Red, yellow and blue are 4, 6 and 1,
    and 4 ⊕ 6 ⊕ 1 = 3. That isn't 0, so they don't lie on one line. Three points
    off one line are a basis, and every colour mixes from them (red ⊕ yellow =
    green). That ties to WordNet's primary color synset.

  If you meant 0x40 rather than 4, that's the one bit the exchange trades (127 ⊕
  63). Tell me which, and I can add the RGB numbering to the colour index next
  to the hues.

❯ i can see a reference point to research but i got those numbers from the back

  know what that means but it follws the pattern also a game called
  open-adventure dooes but im still trying to understand how before why.  lets

● Those numbers are almost certainly the food dyes: FD&C Red No. 40, Yellow No.
  6 and Blue No. 1, the US-certified colourings printed on ingredient lists. The
  numbers are catalogue numbers, given in the order the dyes were listed. They
  don't come from any colour arithmetic, so their fit to RGB (red 4, yellow 6,
  blue 1) is a coincidence. Red is 40, not 4, and 40 doesn't fit the pattern.
  Your scepticism was right.

  What does hold is the colour mixing on the plane (red ⊕ green = yellow), and
  that comes from the plane, not the wrapper.

  Now the Commons story engine. Working through how Light Garden's passages land
  on the plane, one thing can be derived rather than chosen. The square's plane
  is cyclic: line i is {1,2,4} + (i − 1). So turning the plane by a seventh of
  a turn (51.4°) carries each line onto the next. That's the rotation the golden
  test checks: rotating by 51.4° shifts the matrix by one place. The passages'
  angles split into two families, and the two families take different roles:
  - Sevenths (0°, 51.4°, …): line i at (i − 1) × 51.4°. These are the story
    passages.
  - Eighths (45°, 90°, …): the decision points. Eighth k is point k.

  Building the passage index from the canon files:

● The story is committed as 8e963e9, not pushed:
  - Where it runs: on the map, the selected character walks Light Garden's canon
    on the plane.
  - The two kinds of passage: passages at sevenths of a turn stand on the 7
    lines, and those at eighths on the 7 points.
  - A step: from a line, choose one of its 3 points (a decision passage there),
    then one of the 2 other lines through it, or press by K to let K choose.
  - Tests: all 218 that ran pass; the 6 board tests were skipped because the
    board serials weren't set.

  Two clues came out of building it:
  1. Every two lines meet in exactly one point, so any line of the story is one
     step from any other.
  2. K alone can't finish the story. A walk by K closes into a loop of 8, 16 or
     24 steps. Only about a third of seeds and starting lines ever reach all
     seven lines; the rest stay inside 3 to 6. Reaching the rest takes a choice
     from outside, your "all change outside the system".

  On your question, the index to XNOR:
  - A colour with itself (red red, blue blue, yellow yellow):
    - XOR is 0: the null, nothing, black.
    - XNOR is all ones: 7 in the 3-bit colours (white), and 127 in 7 bits, which
      is DEL, the full plane 0x7F.
    - This holds for any literal at all. A thing XORed with itself is always 0,
      and XNORed with itself always the full mask, whatever its own number is.
      So the index to XNOR a tag with itself is 127, the value the exchange's
      buffer is filled with (I-133).
  - Two different colours (3-bit, red 4, green 2, blue 1):
    - XOR is their mix, the third point on their line: red ⊕ yellow = 4 ⊕ 6 = 2,
      green.
    - XNOR is the mix's complement, 7 ⊕ the mix: XNOR(red, yellow) = 7 ⊕ 2 = 5,
      magenta.
    - Complementing pairs each colour with its opposite:

  ┌────────┬───────┬─────────────────┬───────┐
  │ Colour │ Index │ XNOR complement │ Index │
  ├────────┼───────┼─────────────────┼───────┤
  │ red    │ 4     │ cyan            │ 3     │
  ├────────┼───────┼─────────────────┼───────┤
  │ green  │ 2     │ magenta         │ 5     │
  ├────────┼───────┼─────────────────┼───────┤
  │ blue   │ 1     │ yellow          │ 6     │
  └────────┴───────┴─────────────────┴───────┘

  - Those are the complementary colours, and complementary color is one of the
    synsets directly under chromatic color in the 144.

  So XOR finds the third colour on a line, and XNOR finds the colour across from
  it. The dye numbers (40, 6, 1) don't take part in this. Only the colours'
  3-bit indices do.

❯ in space vector we described in 200 i mean like hilbert space

● Bash(git add src/testbed/i-series.test.ts && git commit -q -m "I-series test:
      count every three-digit record (the filter stopped at I-199)…)  ⎿  (No output)

● Committed as 1afadbf. The test's filter only matched records numbered I-100 to
  I-199, so I-200 was being left out. Now it counts every three-digit record,
  and all 78 I-series tests pass.

  On the coincidences:
  - 200 and 0x20: I-200's number comes from its place in the sequence; it's
    simply the 200th record. As a number, 200 isn't 0x20 (which is 32), so that
    link is in the digits only, and I'd treat it as a coincidence.
  - (2, 0, 0) as a vector: read as a vector in 3D, it lies along the first axis,
    with length² 2² = 4.

  The Hilbert space reading meets I-200 at one exact point:
  - In a Hilbert space (Euclidean ℝⁿ is the finite-dimensional case), the inner
    product is positive. The only vector of length 0 is the zero vector, so (2,
    0, 0) has length 2 and can't vanish.
  - E8 and the Leech lattice live in Hilbert spaces, ℝ⁸ and ℝ²⁴.
  - II₂₅,₁ is not a Hilbert space. It's Lorentzian: 25 positive directions and 1
    negative, so the inner product can be zero for a vector that isn't zero.
  - Conway's vector w = (0, 1, …, 24 | 70) is exactly such a vector, nonzero but
    of length 0. The cannonball number (0² + … + 24² = 70²) is what makes that
    possible.
  - So the Leech lattice comes out of the Lorentzian lattice: taking everything
    perpendicular to that null direction and folding the direction itself away
    (w⊥/w) leaves an honest 24-dimensional Hilbert-space lattice. A Hilbert
    space has no such null vector to fold away.

  For the avatars, that suggests each agent's avatar is a generator vector in a
  Hilbert space. The Lorentzian lattice is the larger frame where the null
  direction lives. Moving between the two is the step from the 24 dimensions to
  the 26 (24 + 2).

  What the tests in src/testbed/space.test.ts check now:
  - The values: the XOR file exchanges 127 for 63, and the XNOR file 63 for 127.
    They differ by one bit, 0x40.
  - The index: frame f works on byte 60 ⊕ f. Frame 0 lands on byte 60 and frame
    60 on byte 0, and over 128 frames every one of the 128 bytes is hit exactly
    once.
  - Every frame: frames 0–127 each read 127 and write 63 at their byte. After
    them all 128 bytes are written, and frames 128–255 turn every byte back
    to 127.
  - The compare: if something else already wrote a byte, the exchange reads
    what's there and writes nothing.
  - The Proxy: assigning gate.frame = n runs the exchange through the Proxy,
    Reflect and Catch. An index out of range comes back as a RangeError value
    instead of a throw.

  Frame 120 itself isn't asserted alone, but it's covered by the every-frame
  check. 60 ⊕ 120 = 68, and 120 is in the XOR half, so frame 120 exchanges 127
  for 63 at byte 68.

❯ yes excusme the 63 and 127, i say that because everything above 64 bits has
  to pass through that point, no excuse me ts 32 bytes i just leanered so i
  conversion is my biggest failing right now and DeepSeek is offline and cant
  correct me anymore

● I can check conversions for you while DeepSeek is offline. Here's how these
  numbers relate. A bit is one 0 or 1, and a byte is 8 bits.

  ┌────────┬──────┬───────┬─────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┐
  │        │ bits │ bytes │  largest value it   │ last position (counting   │
  │        │      │       │        holds        │          from 0)          │
  ├────────┼──────┼───────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ 6 bits │ 6    │ ¾     │ 63 (0b111111)       │ 5                         │
  ├────────┼──────┼───────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ 7 bits │ 7    │ ⅞     │ 127 (0b1111111)     │ 6                         │
  ├────────┼──────┼───────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ 1 byte │ 8    │ 1     │ 255                 │ 7                         │
  ├────────┼──────┼───────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ 64     │ 64   │ 8     │ 2⁶⁴ − 1             │ 63                        │
  │ bits   │      │       │                     │                           │
  ├────────┼──────┼───────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ 128    │ 128  │ 16    │ 2¹²⁸ − 1            │ 127                       │
  │ bits   │      │       │                     │                           │
  ├────────┼──────┼───────┼─────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ 256    │ 256  │ 32    │ 2²⁵⁶ − 1            │ 255                       │
  │ bits   │      │       │                     │                           │
  └────────┴──────┴───────┴─────────────────────┴───────────────────────────┘

  So 63 and 127 show up two ways, and both fit what you're saying:
  - As values: 63 is 6 bits all set and 127 is 7 bits all set. In the exchange
    they trade by one bit, the 7th (0x40).
  - As positions: in a 64-bit word the bits are numbered 0 to 63, so bit 63 is
    the last bit of 64 bits. Anything bigger than 64 bits has to carry past
    position 63, which is your "everything above 64 bits passes through that
    point". Likewise bit 127 is the last bit of 128 bits.

  And 32 bytes = 256 bits. Two places in your work already use exactly that
  size:
  - The 24-word identity: a 24-word mnemonic holds 32 bytes of entropy (256
    bits), with an 8-bit check.
  - The exchange's buffer: it's 128 bytes = 1,024 bits, with byte positions 0
    to 127. That's why 7 bits are enough to name any byte, and why 60 ⊕ frame
    stays inside it.

  A rule of thumb for conversions:
  - bytes ↔ bits: multiply by 8, or divide by 8.
  - n bits: the largest value is 2ⁿ − 1, and the last position is n − 1.
    Positions count from 0, so 64 bits end at position 63.

  Send me any number you want converted or checked and I'll run it.

❯ the readining the 200 as idex pairs would be 20,0 in absence of a uppercase
  literal reference

● Read that way it closes neatly, and the 20 turns up either way you read it.

  Without an uppercase literal, 200 read as index pairs is (20, 0). As byte
  pairs in hex:
  - 0x20 is the space, the printable null: your three nulls in omi---imo were
    spaces, 0x20.
  - 0x00 is NUL, the control null.

  The two nulls differ by exactly one bit, 0x20, the same case bit as A/a and
  dot 6 in braille (I-196). So (20, 0) is the null written twice, once printable
  and once as a control code.

  With an uppercase literal, 200 as a base-36 tag is 5K: 200 = 5 × 36 + 20, and
  K is the digit 20. So the tag of I-200 carries the 20 as its last digit.

  One caution on conversions: 200 itself, as a decimal number in hex, is 0xC8
  (1100 1000), not 0x20. The (20, 0) reading only works when the digits are
  taken as pairs, not as one number.

  bigint and unicode as the all numbers are indices

● Running the conversions through BigInt and Unicode directly, so the numbers
  here are computed, not recalled:

● Bash(node -e '
      const
      r=(s,b)=>[...s].reduce((v,c)=>v*BigInt(b)+BigInt(parseInt(c,36)),0n);…)
  ⎿  (No output)

● BigInt is the right carrier for this. It's an index with no width limit, so
  nothing wraps or flips sign when it passes position 63. Here's what the run
  shows.

  The two zeros:
  - 0n is the numeric null, of type bigint.
  - 0u is the character null, String.fromCodePoint(0) = "\u0000" (NUL).
  - The printable null, 0x20, is the space.

  Your "point everything above 64 bits passes through", computed:
  - 2⁶³, the value with only bit 63 set, as a signed 64-bit integer is
    −9,223,372,036,854,775,808: the sign flips there.
  - As unsigned 64-bit it's 9,223,372,036,854,775,808.
  - One past 64 bits, 2⁶⁴, wrapped back to 64 bits is 0n: it falls back to the
    null.
  - As a BigInt it stays exactly 2⁶⁴ = 18,446,744,073,709,551,616. BigInt passes
    straight through the point where fixed-width integers break.

  "200" read as an index in each base:

  ┌──────┬───────┬─────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Base │ Value │       Already in the work       │
  ├──────┼───────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 8    │ 128   │ the exchange buffer's 128 bytes │
  ├──────┼───────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 10   │ 200   │ the record number               │
  ├──────┼───────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 16   │ 512   │ E8's tag (0x200)                │
  ├──────┼───────┼─────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 36   │ 2592  │                                 │
  └──────┴───────┴─────────────────────────────────┘

  So the same digits name the buffer in octal and E8 in hex. The base is the
  reference that says which.

  Sizes that already fit:
  - Tags: a whole 8-character tag fits safely. The largest, ZZZZZZZZ, is
    2,821,109,907,455, about 42 bits, inside ordinary JavaScript numbers' exact
    range (53 bits). METATRON is 1,756,258,266,983 (41 bits) and converts back
    to METATRON exactly.
  - Unicode: every code point fits in 21 bits; the largest is U+10FFFF.
  - So tags and characters never reach position 63. Only BigInt values cross it.

  Modelling every number as an index then comes down to two rules:
  - Carry indices as BigInt: no width limit, so no wrap or sign flip.
  - Narrow at the edge: convert to a fixed width (BigInt.asUintN(64, x) for 64
    bits, a code point for a character) only where a format needs it. That's the
    one place a conversion can go wrong, so it's the one place to check.

● I tested the idea, and its parts hold. It's committed as e42bd01, not pushed.
  All 220 tests that ran pass, with 6 skipped (the live board tests, which need
  the board serials set).

  1. XOR and XNOR over /A-Z0-9/ sort the ASCII table. Uppercase letters and
  digits sit in ASCII columns 1–2. XORing two of them never lands back there:

  ┌────────────────┬───────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┐
  │      Pair      │       XOR lands in        │    7-bit XNOR lands in    │
  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ letter, letter │ control codes (0x00–0x1F) │ lowercase (0x60–0x7F)     │
  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ digit, digit   │ control codes (0x00–0x0F) │ lowercase (0x70–0x7F)     │
  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
  │ letter, digit  │ lowercase (0x60–0x7F)     │ control codes (0x00–0x1F) │
  └────────────────┴───────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────┘

  So combining the uppercase tags gives exactly the system's column (controls)
  and the language's column (lowercase). That's at least consistent with your
  reading of English as a mnemonic layer over rules: the capitals generate the
  other two layers.

  2. The 65,536-pixel mask: each pixel is a pair of bytes, coloured by its order
  under K with the constant replicated (0x1D1D).
  - The orders: 8 indices have order 1, 56 order 2, 4,032 order 4 and 61,440
    order 8.
  - The fixed eight come in byte-swapped pairs, such as 0x41EB ↔ 0xEB41.
  - The order-4 indices form a diamond lattice across the whole image.
  - The 1,296 tag pairs sit in the upper left, gold where their XOR is a control
    code and blue where it's lowercase. The image is on the map page.

  3. Tracing each agent from its margins:
  - How it works: each of an agent's 16 files XORs its place (0–15) into a
    65,536 buffer at the agent's name pairs (ME, TA, TR, ON for METATRON), with
    the complement going into an XNOR buffer.
  - An intact set: because 0 ⊕ 1 ⊕ … ⊕ 15 = 0, an intact set traces to all
    zeros.
  - A change: in the test, rewriting one file's margin lights exactly the
    agent's four pairs, with the size of the change (5 ⊕ 6 = 3).
  - What that means: this is the scrub done from the margins, so the buffer
    shows who changed what without any hashing.

  Not done yet: the trace isn't fed live from the agents in the Commons. Right
  now it reads the files on disk.

❯ each one should be able to be test through oflscreen canvas, offline audio
  context, with a panner and listener node on for each avatar in an xr space
  and then we can use video elements to witness the interaction of the 2 hesse
  diagrams as the stereo panner node listener and the 14 fanoplanes can be
  filter or gain nodes, that way the agents can automate the narrrative and we
  can see if we can replay and scurb and modify the sceene with uppercase
  titile/identity/index terms

● That turns each avatar into a sound graph you can render, replay and scrub.
  Here's how I'd map it, so the wiring follows the plane:
  - Seven points: each Fano point is a voice at its note from Light Garden's
    sound triangle (A–G, from 440 Hz), through a gain node: the 7 point icons.
  - Seven lines: each is a filter node. Each point feeds the 3 lines through it,
    so the graph is the plane's 21 point–line incidences, the middle row of the
    Hasse diagram.
  - Two Hasse diagrams: they are the stereo panner, XOR (read) to the left and
    XNOR (write) to the right. The pan comes from the exchange's byte at each
    frame.
  - Each avatar: gets a panner node at its place in the XR space, heard by one
    listener at the centre.
  - The narrative: an agent's story walk becomes the automation. Each step
    raises its point's gain and its line's filter, at a fixed time.
  - Replay and scrub: the score is fixed and rendered offline, so a replay gives
    the same samples and any span can be re-rendered.
  - Commands: a scene is changed with uppercase terms such as METATRON P2 L6:
    the agent, then a point, then a line.

  First the score, as plain data so it can be tested without a browser:

● Bash(cat > space/src/sound.ts <<'EOF'
      // THE SOUND: an agent's set of 16 as an audio graph (score.ts is the
      plan)…)
  ⎿  Created space/src/sound.ts (+52 -0)
       1 +// THE SOUND: an agent's set of 16 as an audio graph (score.ts 
         +is the plan)                                                   
       2 +//                                                             
       3 +// In any BaseAudioContext, offline for testing and replay, liv
         +e for the XR                                                   
       4 +// space: 7 oscillators at the points' notes, each through its 
         +gain (the 7                                                    
       5 +// point icons) into the 3 line filters through it (the 7 line 
         +icons, band-pass,                                              
       6 +// each followed by its gain), all into one stereo panner (the 
         +2 Hasse                                                        
       7 +// diagrams: XOR read left, XNOR write right), into the avatar'
         +s panner node at                                               
       8 +// its place in the space, heard by the context's listener. The
         + score's events                                                
       9 +// are set on the parameters at their times, so a render is fix
         +ed by the walk:                                                
      10 +// the same walk, the same samples, and a rendered buffer can b
         +e replayed and                                                 
      11 +// scrubbed exactly.                                           
      12 +import { WIRES, noteOf, lineFrequency, LOW, type Event } from '
         +./score.ts';                                                   
      13 +                                                               
      14 +export type AgentGraph = { output: AudioNode; params: Map<strin
         +g, AudioParam>; sources: OscillatorNode[] };                   
      15 +                                                               
      16 +/** Build one avatar's graph in ctx, at a place in the space; c
         +onnect it to dest. */                                          
      17 +export function buildAgent(ctx: BaseAudioContext, at: [number, 
         +number, number], dest: AudioNode = ctx.destination): AgentGraph
         + {                                                             
      18 +  const params = new Map<string, AudioParam>();                
      19 +  const hasse = new StereoPannerNode(ctx, { pan: -1 });        
      20 +  params.set('HASSE.pan', hasse.pan);                          
      21 +  const place = new PannerNode(ctx, { panningModel: 'equalpower
         +', distanceModel: 'inverse', positionX: at[0], positionY: at[1]
         +, positionZ: at[2] });                                         
      22 +  hasse.connect(place).connect(dest);                          
      23 +  const lines = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7].map((l) => {             
      24 +    const filter = new BiquadFilterNode(ctx, { type: 'bandpass'
         +, frequency: lineFrequency(l), Q: 1 });                        
      25 +    const gain = new GainNode(ctx, { gain: LOW });             
      26 +    params.set(`L${l}.gain`, gain.gain);                       
      27 +    filter.connect(gain).connect(hasse);                       
      28 +    return filter;                                             
      29 +  });

❯ we can set the reset to animation frame to auto restotre a state using a
  Proxy and Reflect at the unncanny Catch 22 index

● I've worked out that the Catch-22 exchange occurs at frame 42 (since 60 ⊕ 22 =
  42), where the gate restores all 42 previously written bytes from its
  captured literal instead of exchanging.

● Bash(python3 - <<'EOF'
      p='space/src/exchange.ts'; s=open(p).read()…)
  ⎿  Updated space/src/exchange.ts (+23 -4)
      20  // gate.frame = n is the hand-over: the set trap performs the w
          rite with
      21  // Reflect, then runs the exchange at 60 ^ n inside Catch, so t
          he outcome comes
      22  // back as a value, { ok, value } or { ok: false, error }, neve
          r as a throw.
      23 +//                                                             
      24 +// And the reset: "we can set the reset to animation frame to a
         +uto restore a                                                  
      25 +// state using a Proxy and Reflect at the uncanny Catch 22 inde
         +x". The gate                                                   
      26 +// keeps the state's literal, the state as it stood when the ga
         +te was made.                                                   
      27 +// When the index handed over is 22 (frame 42 in each half: 60 
         +XOR 22 = 42),                                                  
      28 +// the trap does not exchange: it restores every byte from the

❯ the Catch of the CATCH 22 is that iit literallay has the 0x20 space character
  when binary converts to octal

❯ at the 11th index

● The reset logic checks out — it correctly restores 42 bytes at frame 42 and
  fires again at frame 170 in the XNOR half. I'll commit this now.

● Bash(git add space/src/exchange.ts src/testbed/space.test.ts && git commit -q
      -F - <<'EOF'
      The reset at the Catch-22 index: the gate restores its literal at index
      22…)
  ⎿  (No output)

● The reset is committed as ab1944a, not pushed.

  I checked your Catch and the 11th index across the bases. Here's what holds:
  - The space is literally in 22, read as a byte. 0x22 is 0b0010 0010, and its
    high bit is 0x20, the space bit. 0x22 is the double-quote character ("),
    space plus 2.
  - In octal, 0x22 is 42. So reading 22 as hex and writing it in octal gives
    exactly the frame where the reset fires, 42. That's the same 42 as 60 ⊕ 22,
    reached by a different route. For comparison, the space itself is 0x20 =
    0o40.
  - Byte 22 is the 11th index. Taken as 16-bit pairs, bytes 22 and 23 are pair
    11 (22 ÷ 2), counting from 0. So the Catch-22 byte is the 11th index of the
    pairs, the same 11th index as the last character of hello, world.

  A caution on one step: 22 as a decimal number is 0o26 in octal, not 42. The 42
  only appears when 22 is read as hex first (0x22 → 0o42). So the chain holds
  only through that reading: the digits 22, taken as a byte, carry the space bit
  and land on frame 42.

❯ can you check to see if the tag literal CATCH 22 is a tag or the tag literal
  i may spell thks wrong but CAPTCHA is atag liter when xor and xnlr the binary
  to octal conversion

● Bash(node -e '
      const TAG=/^[A-Z0-9]{1,8}$/;…)
  ⎿  (No output)

● Checked with the tag pattern /^[A-Z0-9]{1,8}$/:

  ┌──────────┬────────────────────────────────────┐
  │          │            Is it a tag?            │
  ├──────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ CATCH 22 │ no: the space (0x20) isn't allowed │
  ├──────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ CATCH-22 │ no: the hyphen isn't allowed       │
  ├──────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ CATCH22  │ yes, 7 characters                  │
  ├──────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ CAPTCHA  │ yes, 7 characters                  │
  └──────────┴────────────────────────────────────┘

  So the space is literally the catch: it's the one character that stops CATCH
  22 from being a tag.

  CATCH22 and CAPTCHA XORed and XNORed letter by letter (7-bit XNOR, values in
  octal):

  ┌───────┬─────────┬─────────┬──────────────────────────┬───────────┐
  │ Place │ CATCH22 │ CAPTCHA │           XOR            │   XNOR    │
  ├───────┼─────────┼─────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ 0     │ C 0o103 │ C 0o103 │ 0o000 NUL                │ 0o177 DEL │
  ├───────┼─────────┼─────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ 1     │ A 0o101 │ A 0o101 │ 0o000 NUL                │ 0o177 DEL │
  ├───────┼─────────┼─────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ 2     │ T 0o124 │ P 0o120 │ 0o004 EOT                │ 0o173 {   │
  ├───────┼─────────┼─────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ 3     │ C 0o103 │ T 0o124 │ 0o027 ETB                │ 0o150 h   │
  ├───────┼─────────┼─────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ 4     │ H 0o110 │ C 0o103 │ 0o013 = 11, vertical tab │ 0o164 t   │
  ├───────┼─────────┼─────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ 5     │ 2 0o062 │ H 0o110 │ 0o172 z                  │ 0o005 ENQ │
  ├───────┼─────────┼─────────┼──────────────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ 6     │ 2 0o062 │ A 0o101 │ 0o163 s                  │ 0o014 FF  │
  └───────┴─────────┴─────────┴──────────────────────────┴───────────┘

  - The shared start: CA XORs to null twice, and XNORs to 0o177 = 127 (DEL)
    twice, the value the exchange's buffer is filled with.
  - Letter against letter (places 2–4) gives control codes, and digit against
    letter (places 5–6) gives lowercase. That's exactly the column rule from the
    trace check.
  - Place 4 gives 11: H ⊕ C = 0o013 = 0x0B = 11, the 11th index again, as the
    vertical tab.

  As whole base-36 numbers, CATCH22 = 26,775,340,634 and CAPTCHA =
  26,769,409,390. They share their first three octal digits (0o307…). Their XOR
  is 8,094,516, which reads as the tag 4THRO in base 36.

● CATCH 22 now works as a command pair. It's committed as 6cd8fc9, not pushed.

  - Parsing: with the space, CATCH 22 is two tags, CATCH and 22, so it parses as
    a verb and an index. CATCH n sets where the gate's reset fires, for any
    index from 0 to 127.
  - Through the Proxy: the gate takes the new index through its Proxy. It
    refuses an index outside its 128 bytes, the same way it refuses writes to
    anything but the frame.
  - On the map: there's now a terms box under the story:
    - CATCH 22: the reset fires at byte 22, which is frame 42 of each half.
    - CATCH 60: moves it to frame 0.
    - METATRON P2 L6: switches to METATRON and takes that step in the story,
      through point 2 to line 6.
    - A bad command: comes back as a message saying why, such as "P1 is not on
      L3".
  - Tests: all 224 that ran pass, with 6 skipped for want of the board serials.
    I didn't drive the terms box itself in a browser.

  - The video witness: an OffscreenCanvas drawing each frame, captured to a

● The recording checks out — a 4.4s WebM with video/audio at the expected

● I'm implementing the routing as a small, testable pure function: the agent is
  derived from the Host header or first path segment with remaining segments as
  terms, GET returns the route and messages, and POST applies the terms via the
  bridge (e.g. moving METATRON through point 2 to line 6).

         +text: string } | null {

● Bash(git add space/src/map.ts && git commit -q -m "Map: terms said on the

  ⎿  (No output)

● The witness and tag routing are done. The commits are 97a8b48 and c7fcf98, not
  pushed. All 227 tests that ran pass, with 6 skipped (the live board tests,
  which need the board serials set).

  The witness:
  - Recording: "record the witness" on the map draws the selected agent's next 8
    steps on an OffscreenCanvas: the plane with the step's point and line lit,
    the gate's 128 bytes, and the read/write pan. It records the frames together
    with the agent's live sound.
  - Checked headless: the result is a 4.4-second WebM with both VP8 video and
    Opus audio. It plays in a video element where you can replay and scrub.
  - The drawing: the plane's point positions are derived from the plane itself,
    not placed by hand. So every line is drawn straight except the one that's
    the circle.

  Agents by tag:
  - The URL: //metatron.localhost:8743/P2/L6 (agent in the host) or
    /METATRON/P2/L6 (agent in the path) reaches METATRON on the bridge.
  - Reading: GET returns the route and what that agent has said.
  - Taking a step: POST says the terms on the bridge, and the map takes the
    step. Because the bridge replays its history when a page connects, a freshly
    opened map replays every command posted so far.
  - Folding: the host folds to lowercase, so metatron arrives, which is the tag
    XOR 0x20 letter by letter. It's read back in uppercase.

  The port as the 4-bit parity: this works out neatly. A port is 16 bits, four
  nibbles, so XORing them gives a 4-bit check:
  - The bridge's port: 8743 = 0x2227, so its parity is 2 ⊕ 2 ⊕ 2 ⊕ 7 = 5.
  - The tags' parities (the XOR of all the nibbles of their letters): METATRON
    11, SOLOMON 6, SOLON 4, ASABIYAH 8, ENOCH 11, SPEAKER 14, GENESIS 4,
    OBSERVER 8, CATCH 8.
  - The fit: in the block 0x2220–0x222F (ports 8736–8751), the last nibble alone
    sets the parity, so the 16 ports cover the 16 checks exactly once. An
    agent's port would be the one whose parity equals its tag's. METATRON's
    would be 0x2229 = 8745, and SOLOMON's 0x2224 = 8740.
  - The mismatch check: a URL whose port doesn't match its host's parity would
    be refused, the same 4-bit check the 12-word identity uses.
  - Shared ports: agents with the same parity share a port (METATRON and ENOCH
    both get 8745), and the host tells them apart.

  Shall I make the bridge listen on those ports and refuse a host on the wrong
  one?

❯ so the word net of the colorrs with unicde gives them natural language

● Unicode names its characters in uppercase English, so a character's name is
  already a run of tag-like terms (LARGE RED SQUARE). Checking how WordNet's 144
  colour words meet those names:

● Bash(python3 - <<'EOF'
      import json, unicodedata, sys, re…)
  ⎿  (No output)
  ⎿  (timeout 2m)

● They do, and Unicode's names make it concrete. Every character's name is an
  uppercase English phrase, LARGE RED SQUARE, and each word of eight letters or
  fewer is a tag. So a Unicode name is already a sentence of tag literals.

  The nine basic colours each have a square and a circle:

  ┌────────┬────────────┬────────────┐
  │ Colour │   Square   │   Circle   │
  ├────────┼────────────┼────────────┤
  │ red    │ 🟥 U+1F7E5 │ 🔴 U+1F534 │
  ├────────┼────────────┼────────────┤
  │ orange │ 🟧 U+1F7E7 │ 🟠 U+1F7E0 │
  ├────────┼────────────┼────────────┤
  │ yellow │ 🟨 U+1F7E8 │ 🟡 U+1F7E1 │
  ├────────┼────────────┼────────────┤
  │ green  │ 🟩 U+1F7E9 │ 🟢 U+1F7E2 │
  ├────────┼────────────┼────────────┤
  │ blue   │ 🟦 U+1F7E6 │ 🔵 U+1F535 │
  ├────────┼────────────┼────────────┤
  │ purple │ 🟪 U+1F7EA │ 🟣 U+1F7E3 │
  ├────────┼────────────┼────────────┤
  │ brown  │ 🟫 U+1F7EB │ 🟤 U+1F7E4 │
  ├────────┼────────────┼────────────┤
  │ black  │ ⬛ U+2B1B  │ ⚫ U+26AB  │
  ├────────┼────────────┼────────────┤
  │ white  │ ⬜ U+2B1C  │ ⚪ U+26AA  │
  └────────┴────────────┴────────────┘

  Most sit in one run of twelve code points, U+1F7E0 to U+1F7EB. Red and blue
  circles come from earlier blocks (U+1F534, U+1F535), and black and white from
  older ones still.

  Across all 143,041 named characters:
  - Coverage: 26 of the 144 colour synsets' words appear in some name. Black
    (322 names) and white (289) appear most, because Unicode uses them for
    filled and hollow shapes; red appears in 20, blue 9, green 8.
  - Same letters, other things: some matches are the same uppercase letters
    naming something else. TAN is a Georgian letter, DUN a Hangul syllable, BONE
    and WINE CJK radicals. That's your mnemonic point exactly: the letters are
    one tag, and only the context, the synset, says which meaning is in play.

  So each colour agent can carry its natural language from two sides:
  - WordNet: the meaning (its synset, gloss and route up to entity).
  - Unicode: a glyph and an uppercase name, inherited from the nearest ancestor
    that has one, the way hues are now. Scarlet would take 🔴 from red.

  Should I add the glyph and the Unicode name to each colour agent, flagging the
  homographs?

❯ yes all these sugggestions seem necessary

● I'll do all of them in turn:
  1. Glyphs: each colour agent gets its Unicode glyph and name, with homographs
     flagged.
  2. The port as the parity: the bridge listens on the 16 ports of its block,
     and an agent reached by host must come in on the port whose parity matches
     its tag.
  3. Live sound in the 3D Commons: the characters as avatars, each with its own
     panner.

  First the glyphs. Node has no Unicode name table, so a small Python step
  writes it out. A match counts as a homograph when the character is a letter or
  syllable (Unicode category L…), because then the colour word is only its
  name, as with Georgian TAN:

● The colour glyphs and the port parity are committed as b434daa, not pushed.

  On the Universal POS: polytron doesn't hold it, but your tetragrammatron
  design docs already place it. The S-P-O filter has a home:
  - OMI_ADDRESS_DERIVATION_PROTOCOL.md: the semantic path is Subject, Predicate,
    Object, Modality, "backed by UPOS tags, universal features, WordNet
    synsets, Prolog facts". Its example is
    omi-<frame>/human/asks/god/question-imo, whose subject is human, predicate
    asks, object god and modality question.
  - 4.1_IPv6_FRAME_MAPPING.md: the address's fourth 16-bit segment, S[3], is the
    UPOS tag.
  - OMI_ADDRESSING.md: lists "UPOS context ports" among what Omi-Log may use,
    beside ip4-notation, ip6-notation and the omi-/-imo blocks.

  And the sets are ready-made, as you say:
  - Universal POS has 17 tags: ADJ ADP ADV AUX CCONJ DET INTJ NOUN NUM PART PRON
    PROPN PUNCT SCONJ SYM VERB X. All are uppercase and 5 letters or fewer, so
    every one is already a tag. It's 16 named parts plus X, "other", the
    catch-all.
  - WordNet already gives each word its part of speech (n, v, a/s, r), which
    maps straight onto NOUN, VERB, ADJ and ADV.
  - The filter would pass a triple when the subject and object are NOUN, PROPN
    or PRON and the predicate is VERB or AUX. The Universal Features (Number,
    Person, Tense and so on) refine the predicate: asks is third person,
    singular, present.

  I'd write the filter as a check first. One choice decides where it's used
  first:
  1. The story: each canon passage's sentences give S-P-O triples that stand on
     the passage's line. The story would then carry its own subjects and
     objects.
  2. The bridge: each message is read as omi-<frame>/S/P/O/M-imo, with the UPOS
     tag in segment S[3]. Agents' messages would then be routed by their parts
     of speech.

  Either way, WordNet lives outside the repo (in ~/Downloads/WNprolog-3.0, and
  as a tarball in Light Garden). So the build would take a word list from it
  into public/, as it did for the colours. Which first?

● I read the project through: the README, the types and the engines, about
  17,800 lines of TypeScript. The decision trees aren't in it.
  - The axioms: the README says every operation is checked against "Hilbert's 21
    axioms", but the code defines three of them: I1 (incidence), II1 (order)
    and III1 (congruence). The engines name only INCIDENCE_I1, ORDER_II3 and
    CONGRUENCE_III1.
  - The validator is a stub. AxiomValidator.validate returns valid: true,
    confidence: 0.8 for any input, with the comment "Simplified validation".
  - No tree: there's no decision tree, branching or axiom ordering anywhere in
    the source, the scripts, or the projective_completion_paper.md.
  - The 21 is a label: it appears as vec21 (a 21-dimensional vector) and as
    peers: 21 with the comment "Based on Hilbert's 21 axioms".

  Your "I 20 1" is historically right. Hilbert's Foundations of Geometry (1899)
  first gave 21 axioms:
  - 8 of incidence, 4 of order, 5 of congruence, 1 of parallels and 2 of
    continuity: that's 20 as later counted, plus one more order axiom.
  - In 1902 E. H. Moore proved that extra order axiom follows from the others,
    so the list became 20. Hilbert's 21 is literally 20 + 1, with the 1 being
    the axiom that turned out redundant.

  These are Hilbert's axioms of geometry, a different work from Hilbert space
  (the function spaces), although both carry his name.

  The 21 also matches the plane we've been using: the Fano plane has 21 pairs of
  points and 21 point-on-line incidences (the 21 wires in the sound graph).

  That makes a real decision tree possible: run Hilbert's axioms, group by
  group, against the Fano plane.
  - Incidence: holds. Two points lie on exactly one line, which is I-198.
  - Order: fails. A finite plane has no "between".
  - Parallels: fails differently. No two lines are parallel, since every two
    meet.

  Each branch is checkable, and the tree would show exactly where a finite,
  closed plane leaves Euclid. Shall I build it, as a check in the vault and
  filling in the missing axioms in AGS, or leave AGS as it is?

● That's the memory tree: five kinds of memory, episodic, semantic, procedural,
  working and meta, each stored with tags and kept per identity. The API's shape
  fits what we've built in /space:

  ┌──────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ autonomous-identity  │                    /space now                    │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ POST /api/identities │ an identity: the entropy as /A-Z0-9/ tags, from  │
  │                      │ the 12 words                                     │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ POST /api/memories   │ a link in the identity's chain: sid = K(sid,     │
  │                      │ pair) over the record                            │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ GET /api/memories    │ the chain replayed, which also names the first   │
  │                      │ changed record                                   │
  ├──────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ the five memory      │ could be tags on each link                       │
  │ kinds                │                                                  │
  └──────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  As tags, four of the kinds fit: EPISODIC, SEMANTIC, WORKING and META.
  PROCEDURAL is 10 letters, over the limit of 8, the same as ASABIYYAH was.

  Under the API, though, the implementation is mostly placeholders, and some of
  it reports things that aren't true. I read the source:
  - "AES-256" encryption is base64. encrypt() returns
    Buffer.from(JSON.stringify(x)).toString("base64") labelled algorithm:
    "AES-256". So the "encrypted" state.json can be read by anyone, and anything
    stored there should be treated as plaintext.
  - Login accepts any password, as long as a username and password are present,
    and authorization always says yes.
  - The fingerprint is the identity's JSON in base64. The example in the spec
    decodes to {"name":"Test AI","type":"ai",…}. That's the literal itself
    rather than a hash, which is close to your doctrine, but it isn't what the
    label claims.
  - The hyperbolic positions are random, and some fall outside the space.
    Coordinates are random in [−1, 1]³, and the norm is then reported as at most
    0.9 without the coordinates being scaled. The spec's own example has a real
    norm of 1.18: outside the unit ball, so not a point of the hyperbolic space
    at all.
  - Embeddings and IDs are random: the embeddings are random 64-number vectors
    and the IDs are random UUIDs. Nothing is derived from the identity.
  - "Hyperbolic neighbours" returns the first n memories stored.

  So the project gives a good API and memory-tree shape, but none of its
  identity or security logic can be relied on as written.

  I'd suggest putting the same endpoints on the bridge, backed by what holds up:
  - /api/identities: the hash-free identity.
  - /api/memories: chain links tagged with their memory kind.
  - Reading back: a chain replay that finds the first changed record.

  That leaves autonomous-identity's code as it is. One choice before I start:
  PROCEDURAL is 10 letters. Should it be shortened to 8 to fit the tag rule
  (e.g. PROCEDUR, or PROCESS), or carried as a lowercase name outside the tags?

  One more is a real bug, not a placeholder: the identity fingerprint collides.
  It's fp_ plus the first 16 base64 characters of the identity's JSON. That
  covers only the first 12 bytes, and {"name":" uses 9 of them. So any two
  identities whose names start with the same 3 letters, such as "My AI" and "My
  Assistant", get the same fingerprint.

  What carries over is the design:
  - The endpoints (identities, memories, learn, health, status).
  - The five memory kinds.
  - The idea that an AI keeps its identity and memories across sessions.

  That's the part I'd rebuild on the bridge with the pieces that hold up:
  - Identity: the entropy as tags. Because it's the whole entropy, no two
    identities can share a name.
  - Memories: chain links tagged by kind.
  - Reading back: replaying the chain finds the first record that changed. That
    gives the integrity the "encryption" was meant to provide, without
    pretending to secrecy.

  If the stored memories also need to be kept secret from people reading the
  files, that's real encryption (Web Crypto's AES-GCM), and it would be a
  separate step.

  The open question from before still stands: PROCEDURAL is 10 letters. Should
  it be shortened to an 8-letter tag (PROCEDUR, or PROCESS), or kept as a
  lowercase name outside the tags? Once you've answered, I'll put the memory
  endpoints on the bridge.

❯ yes exactlty this is what the escape does from B language to C language

● The escape is the same bit move, one column further down. ASCII is four
  columns of 32, and two XORs connect three of them:

  ┌────────┬───────────┬────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┐
  │ Column │   Range   │  What lives there  │           Step down           │
  ├────────┼───────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ 3      │ 0x60–0x7F │ lowercase:         │                               │
  │        │           │ deprecated tags    │                               │
  ├────────┼───────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ 2      │ 0x40–0x5F │ uppercase: tags    │ lowercase → uppercase is XOR  │
  │        │           │                    │ 0x20 (generate)               │
  ├────────┼───────────┼────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ 0      │ 0x00–0x1F │ control codes      │ uppercase → control is XOR    │
  │        │           │                    │ 0x40 (escape)                 │
  └────────┴───────────┴────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┘

  The second step is caret notation:
  - ^J = J ⊕ 0x40 = 0x0A, the newline that B wrote as *n and C as \n.
  - ^M = 0x0D (carriage return).
  - ^[ = 0x1B (ESC).
  - ^@ = 0x00 (NUL).

  The escape takes a printable letter into the control column, the column XOR
  sent letter-with-letter pairs into in the trace.

  C's escape character itself lands on your separators. \ is 0x5C, and 0x5C ⊕
  0x40 = 0x1C, FS. The four characters at the end of the uppercase column step
  down onto the four separators:

  ┌───────────┬──────┬────────┬───────────┐
  │ Character │ Code │ ⊕ 0x40 │ Separator │
  ├───────────┼──────┼────────┼───────────┤
  │ \         │ 0x5C │ 0x1C   │ FS        │
  ├───────────┼──────┼────────┼───────────┤
  │ ]         │ 0x5D │ 0x1D   │ GS        │
  ├───────────┼──────┼────────┼───────────┤
  │ ^         │ 0x5E │ 0x1E   │ RS        │
  ├───────────┼──────┼────────┼───────────┤
  │ _         │ 0x5F │ 0x1F   │ US        │
  └───────────┴──────┴────────┴───────────┘

  So C's escape, taken down one column, is the File Separator, the first of FS
  GS RS US. B's * (0x2A) sits down in the punctuation column instead, and ⊕ 0x40
  only takes it up to lowercase j. That's my reading of why moving the escape
  from * to \ mattered: \ sits right above the separators.

  The functor is committed as 90bdac1, not pushed. All 230 tests that ran pass,
  with 6 skipped (the board tests):
  - Generate: toTag('metatron') gives METATRON. At the type level,
    ToTag<'metatron'> is the literal type 'METATRON', and ToTag<'procedural'> is
    never, refused at compile time.
  - Deprecate: deprecate('METATRON') gives metatron, and generating it again
    gives METATRON back.
  - Digits can't be deprecated: 22 has no case, so numbers stay indices.
  - Hosts: a URL's host holds a tag in its deprecated form.
  - The type checks fail when wrong: I fed the compiler a false claim and it
    refused it.

  PROCEDURAL is the live example: as a word it can't be generated into a tag.
  That's still the open question from before: shorten it to 8 letters, or keep
  it lowercase, as a name that isn't a tag.

● The conflict engine can't run its two main paths, and the one that does run
  doesn't give repeatable results:
  - 25 methods it calls are never defined, including validateArbitrators,
    analyzeDisputeAxiomatically, createArbitrationMatrix and
    createMediationFramework. Arbitration and mediation would fail on their
    first step.
  - Each dispute's vector includes Math.random() ("Entropy component") and the
    dispute's age at the moment it's computed. So the same dispute resolved
    twice gets different resolutions, which an arbitration engine can't afford.
  - Consistency is written in, not checked: consistent: true, confidence: 0.9,
    with the golden ratio (0.618) as a fixed "protection level".

  BASIC, C++ and C# as tags, against B → C:
  - B ⊕ C = 0x01. B and C differ only in the lowest bit, the smallest step there
    is.
  - BASIC is a tag, and it literally runs from B to C: B…C.
  - C++ is "one more than C": C + 1 = D. And C ⊕ D = 0x07, BEL, the Fano line
    {1,3,2}, one of the 16 Hamming codewords in I-193.
  - C++ and C# aren't tags. + and # are exactly the characters a URL treats
    specially:
    - in a path, # starts the fragment, so http://x/C# has the path /C: C# falls
      back to C;
    - C++ has to travel as C%2B%2B.
  - Their tag forms would be CPP and CSHARP.
  - The ++ cancels: + ⊕ + = 0. And dropped one column with ⊕ 0x20, + (0x2B)
    becomes 0x0B, 11, the vertical tab, and # (0x23) becomes 0x03, ETX, end of
    text.

  - The parser, had the Lisp ever answered, returns the same default
    ("Simplified for now").
  - So: the INTJ cognitive stack, its development levels (0.7, 0.6, 0.4, 0.3 …),
    the "shadow" functions, harmony and stability are all constants. Nothing
    about the person changes them. Personal harmony goes the same way: its
    parser and fallback return a default too.

  One risk if this is ever wired up: the answers are spliced into the Lisp
  source as text (:responses ${JSON.stringify(responses)}) and then evaluated.
  Someone's answers could carry code into the evaluator. The answers should be
  passed as data, not as part of the expression.

  What's real here is the vocabulary:
  - The 16 types are a 4-bit code, and each type's opposite is XOR 15.
  - The cognitive functions are an ordered stack: dominant, auxiliary, tertiary,
    inferior, then four shadows.

  An actual assessment would give each of the four letters its own bit, set from
  the answers. That would make the type an index derived from the input rather
  than a default.

❯ the misconception is we have to wire it but the sture is the xor or xnor
  proof that the agents tree themselves based on narrative semantics and syntax

● Understood. Then the 136 undeclared names aren't gaps to fill by hand. They're
  the open places in the structure, and the agents fill them themselves:
  - The types are the literals.
  - The narrative supplies the words, in its syntax and semantics.
  - XOR and XNOR are the proof. A word joins an agent's tree where its tag XORs
    against a literal to 0, the null that shows it holds. XNOR, all ones, is the
    same proof read from the write side.

  So instead of wiring the old projects, I stop at the structure and let the
  derivation do the rest. Saving that so I don't drift back into proposing

  The match does carry one more condition, the tag pattern's length. A word
  longer than 8 letters, such as PROCEDURAL, or a compound type name such as
  IntegrationAbility, can never match, whatever its case. That's why 133 of the
  136 undeclared names can't be filled this way. They'd have to be split into
  words of 8 letters or fewer before the narrative could fill them.

  passage words would go in as data, since the devops project and Light Garden
  aren't part of this repository.

❯ excuse me said that backwards again the conversion from lower to upper
  promotes a suibject to an object
● That changes what the two directions are: case is the word's role in the
  triple.
  - Lowercase is the subject: the word as said, unbound.
  - Uppercase is the object: the tag, the literal it's bound to.
  - Lower → upper promotes the subject to the object.

  And it closes like a triple. In every letter, the lowercase and uppercase
  forms differ by exactly 0x20: m ⊕ M = 0x6D ⊕ 0x4D = 0x20. So for a word s and
  its promoted tag O:
  - s ⊕ O = 0x20 0x20 …, one space per letter.
  - The predicate between a subject and its object is the space.
  - s ⊕ P ⊕ O = 0, with P the spaces, the same closure as the Steiner triples
    (I-198).

  That's your three nulls in omi---imo, which we found to be 0x20: the predicate
  slot holds the space.

  Correcting the record in the code, where I'd written "generate" and
  "deprecate":

● I've recorded it in the code (commit 01c1825, not pushed), with a test that

● The H²GNN code is the most substantial of the old projects. Its hyperbolic
  maths is real Poincaré-ball geometry, Möbius addition and exponential maps,
  not random numbers. But I checked two of its formulas against the standard
  closed form (arcosh(1 + 2|u−v|² / ((1−|u|²)(1−|v|²)))), and both are off:
  - Möbius addition divides by (1 + u·v) once too often. It's right only when
    u·v = 0.
  - The distance is artanh(‖−u ⊕ v‖). The Poincaré distance is **2·**artanh(…).

  For u = (0.3, 0.1), v = (−0.2, 0.4) the true distance is 1.2843, and the
  project's code gives 0.6259, less than half. Rankings by distance mostly
  survive, but every distance value, and anything thresholded on one, is off.
  Separately, its .mcp.json points at
  /home/main/devops/universal-life-protocol/…, which doesn't exist on this
  machine. So those four servers won't start from that config, although the
  servers themselves are here in src/mcp.

  The "transcriber of the semantic web" now has most of its pieces in place:

  ┌─────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┬───────────────────┐

❯ yes but its not the adatp tation if the existence of them is xor and xnor

● I'd half-expected the projection to miss, and it doesn't:
  - All 400 points are in E8. That covers 196 integer points and 204
    half-integer points across 400 paths.
  - The half-integer shift is subtle but sound. Emacs rounds halves to even, so
    a vector with an odd sum shifts by exactly enough to make its sum even.

  Understood: not adapting the code. Read for whether its structures exist as
  literals that XOR and XNOR under the tag toggle:
  - The point exists. Every path lands on a real E8 lattice point, checked.
  - The path is an address of indices. In m/44'/0'/0'/0/0, the numbers have no
    case, so they stay indices whichever way the toggle goes.
  - The root m is a lowercase letter, a subject. It promotes to M, the object,
    by XOR 0x20.
  - The hardening mark ' (0x27), dropped one column with ⊕ 0x20, becomes 0x07,
    BEL: the Fano line {1,3,2}.
  - The point isn't derived from the path. It comes from SHA-256 of the path
    text, so a child and its parent land on unrelated points:
    - parent m/44'/0'/0' → (−7.5, −7.5, 6.5, −1.5, 2.5, −7.5, 6.5, 4.5)
    - child m/44'/0'/0'/0/0 → (−7.5, 6.5, −9.5, −9.5, 4.5, 8.5, 6.5, −1.5)

    The hierarchy is kept only as a stored pointer, not by where the points are.
    So the doc's "delegate in the master's Weyl orbit" test runs on points the
    hash scattered, not on any relation between the paths. Without the hash, the
    path's own indices are the identity, and the relation between parent and
    child is the prefix they share: the edge n-gram from before.

● Bash(sed -n 55,71p omi-files/meta-log/docs/GEOMETRIC_CONSENSUS.md; sed -n

● The geometry is right:
  - Vertex counts: tetrahedron 4, octahedron 6, cube 8, dodecahedron 20, 5-cell
    5, 24-cell 24, truncated tetrahedron 12.
  - Duals: the tetrahedron is self-dual, the cube and octahedron are duals, and
    so are the dodecahedron and icosahedron. The doc's "inverse duality" is
    ordinary duality.

  The thresholds are a different matter:
  - The cube's 4 of 8 (50%) is called a "simple majority", but 4 of 8 is a tie.
    A majority of 8 is 5.
  - The thresholds are assigned, not derived from the shapes. Nothing about an
    octahedron gives 5 of 6, or a dodecahedron 18 of 20.

  There is a standard way to derive a threshold from the count alone, the
  Byzantine quorum. With n participants it tolerates f = ⌊(n−1)/3⌋ faulty ones
  and needs n − f to agree:

  ┌───────────────────────┬─────┬─────────────────┬──────────────────┐
  │         Shape         │  n  │ Doc's threshold │ Byzantine quorum │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Tetrahedron           │ 4   │ 4               │ 3                │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ 5-cell                │ 5   │ 5               │ 4                │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Octahedron            │ 6   │ 5               │ 5 (matches)      │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Cube                  │ 8   │ 4               │ 6                │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Truncated tetrahedron │ 12  │ 12              │ 9                │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Dodecahedron          │ 20  │ 18              │ 14               │
  ├───────────────────────┼─────┼─────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ 24-cell               │ 24  │ 20              │ 17               │
  └───────────────────────┴─────┴─────────────────┴──────────────────┘

  Only the octahedron agrees, so the doc's numbers aren't this rule either.
  They're choices.

  The doc also pins each shape to an RFC 2119 keyword, MUST, SHOULD, MAY,
  MUST_NOT. Your vault deliberately avoids MUST and SHALL: it derives and
  indexes rather than commands. That's the part of this doc that runs against
  the doctrine. The geometry carries over cleanly. The keywords would be read as
  labels, not as orders.

    embeddings, so it can run with no cloud at all.

  One thing has dated: the default model is claude-3-5-haiku-20241022, a model
  from 2024. Older models get retired, and when that one is, calls with this
  default will start failing. The current small Claude model is Haiku 5.5,
  claude-haiku-5-5. Changing it is a one-line defcustom in
  modules/meta-log-llm-anthropic.el, and it's yours to make.

  Read for its structure, the query translation is six steps:
  1. Apply personal vocabulary.
  2. Check the cache.
  3. Expand locally.
  4. Build the query.
  5. Classify the dimension.
  6. Return.

  Step 1 is the tag functor's job in your terms. A person's own words are the
  subjects, and promoting them to the shared vocabulary's tags makes them
  objects the rest of the system can match by XOR. A cache and a local expansion
  then only ever see tags, and a word too long to promote (as with PROCEDURAL
  or CONSCIOUSNESS) is exactly what the LLM step would be needed for.

● I'll verify this with the Math.ceil(vertices * threshold) rounding logic,
  since 5/6 isn't an exact binary fraction and could cause rounding issues.

● Bash(node -e 'for(const [n,want] of
      [[6,5],[24,20],[12,10]]){console.log("n",n,"| ceil(n * 5/6)
      =",Math.ceil(n*(5/6)),"(n * 5/6 =",n*(5/6)+")","| ceil(n * 0.833) =",…)
  ⎿  (No output)

● The arithmetic holds:
  - The counts come out right: ceil(n × 5/6) gives 5, 20 and 10 for 6, 24
    and 12. Every 5/6 shape has a vertex count divisible by 6, so the product
    lands on a whole number.
  - The module stores the exact count: modules/meta-log-geometric-consensus.el
    keeps it next to the ratio, e.g. (consensus . 20) (threshold . 0.833) for
    the 24-cell. The integer is the literal, so rounding can't move it.
  - The tables agree: icosahedron 10 of 12, icosidodecahedron 25 of 30.

  The two points from the last doc carry over:
  - Every 50% tier is a tie, not a majority: the cube's 4 of 8, the 8-cell's 8
    of 16, the truncated cube's 12 of 24.
  - The ratios are the same three in every context (all, 5/6, half). The shapes
    only supply n.

  One claim deserves care: that this "replaces traditional quorum-based systems
  (Raft, Paxos)". Raft and Paxos do more than count agreement. They keep nodes
  agreeing when messages are lost, delayed or reordered, and when a node crashes
  partway through. A threshold says how many agreements are enough. It doesn't
  get those agreements through a faulty network. So the shapes give a
  well-defined threshold, but the consensus still needs a protocol under it.

❯ /home/main/Programs/boxedPINE/omi-files/meta-log/docs/architecture/geometric-
  architecture.md

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

● Bash(sed -n 49,70p docs/architecture/geometric-architecture.md; sed -n 19,30p
      docs/architecture/geometric-architecture.md)
  ⎿  (No output)

● This doc is correct, and its Layer 3 is the Fano plane, though it doesn't say
  so.
  - The BIBD conditions are stated correctly. A balanced incomplete block design
    (v, b, r, k, λ) needs vr = bk (count the incidences both ways), λ(v−1) =
    r(k−1) (balance), and b ≥ v (Fisher's inequality).
  - The Fano plane is the smallest example: (7, 7, 3, 3, 1). It has 7 points and
    7 lines, each point on 3 lines, each line through 3 points, and each pair
    of points on exactly 1 line:
    - 7 × 3 = 7 × 3 = 21, the plane's incidences, and the 21 wires in the sound
      graph.
    - 1 × 6 = 3 × 2.
    - b = v: the plane is symmetric, which is the edge case of Fisher's
      inequality.
  - In the doc's terms: 7 requirements and 7 documents, each document covering 3
    requirements, each requirement in 3 documents, and every pair of
    requirements sharing exactly one document. That's the Steiner triple system
    of I-198.
  - The other geometry: the tetrahedron is 4 fully connected vertices, and the
    cube has 8 vertices and 6 faces (with 12 edges: 8 − 12 + 6 = 2).

  The doc maps documents to blocks and requirements to points. With the lattice
  from today, documents and the tags they share, that can be measured on real
  folders: count v, b, r, k and λ, and see how far the incidence is from
  balanced. The meta-log docs won't be a BIBD, because their r and λ vary
  widely, but the measurement shows which tags carry the balance.

  content addressing test actually relies on, since hashing is supposed to sit
  outside the protocol.

● Bash(cd /home/main/Programs/boxedPINE/omi-files/meta-log/scheme && sed -n
      74,95p substrate/runtime.scm;
      S=/tmp/claude-1001/-home-main-Programs-boxedPINE/aa031b7d-6fd…)
  ⎿  (No output)

● The "hash" is an XOR fold: every character times 31, XORed together. It runs,
  but as an address it collides:
  - Order doesn't count: ab and ba get the same address, 61.
  - Doubled characters cancel: a character appearing twice adds to 0, so aa
    vanishes entirely.

  So any two contents that are anagrams, or that differ by a pair of repeated
  characters, share an address. The test passes because it only checks that an
  address is produced, not that different contents get different ones.

  In your terms, this is a parity check, not an identity. It's the same XOR fold
  as the identity's nibble parity, good for noticing that something changed,
  but it can't say what the content is. Two existing pieces of boxedPINE already
  cover the identity side:
  - The address is the content itself: a tag, or the bytes compared literally by
    the scrub, which XORs against the literal.
  - The order-sensitive carry is the chain: each pair steps K in turn, so ab and
    ba part ways at the first step.

  The rest of the Scheme substrate is sound as far as its tests go: rotate,
  slice and concat are correct, and they're the delta law's own operations.

● Two corrections first.

  SHA-256: there are two different modules here.
  - The E8 module (meta-log-e8.el) does use SHA-256 natively, through Emacs's
    built-in (secure-hash 'sha256 path). That's how a path becomes a point.
  - The Scheme substrate's "content hash" is not SHA. It's the XOR fold I just
    showed, with SHA3 named only in a comment as the plan.

  The phrase: the English nursery rhyme is "Fee-fi-fo-fum". In Adventure,
  though, the magic sequence is FEE FIE FOE FOO, said in that order to get the
  golden eggs back. Checking both, your LOGOS / PATHOS / NOMOS triple, and the
  game's own words:

● Bash(node -e '
      const
      TAG=/^[A-Z0-9]{1,8}$/,hx=v=>v.toString(16).padStart(2,"0"),ch=v=>v>=0x21&&
      v<0x7f?String.fromCharCode(v):v===0x20?"␠":"^"+String.fromCharCode(v^0…)
  ⎿  (No output)
  ⎿  (timeout 1m)

● FEE FIE FOE FOO (all four are tags, three letters each). XORing the four words
  letter by letter:
  - First letters: F ⊕ F ⊕ F ⊕ F = 0, null. Four of the same letter cancel.
  - Second letters: E ⊕ I ⊕ O ⊕ O = 0x0C, form feed (a new page).
  - Third letters: E ⊕ E ⊕ E ⊕ O = 0x0A, line feed (a new line).

  So the chant collapses entirely into the control column: null, new page, new
  line. Its XNOR is DEL, s, u. The rhyme's version, FEE FI FO FUM, gives null,
  0x16 (SYN), 0x08 (backspace).

  LOGOS, PATHOS, NOMOS as S, P, O. All three are tags, and shorter words are
  padded with the space, your null predicate.
  - They don't close as a line: LOGOS ⊕ PATHOS ⊕ NOMOS isn't 0. Letter by letter
    it's R A ^H H O S.
  - LOGOS and NOMOS are nearly the same word. They differ in only two letters:
    L/N (0x02) and G/M (0x0A, line feed). The O, the O and the S all cancel.
  - What would close it: with LOGOS as subject and NOMOS as object, the
    predicate has to be S ⊕ O = STX, null, LF, null, null: "start of text" and a
    line feed, and nothing else. PATHOS is not that predicate.

  So word and law are one line feed apart, and the predicate between them is
  almost all null. Aristotle's classical triad is logos, pathos, ethos. Swapping
  in ETHOS gives YZ[HOS, which doesn't close either.

  The hint page: open-adventure isn't installed on this machine, so I worked
  from the hints as you pasted them. They're puns whose answer is a literal word
  you type:
  - "Say the words you see" means the magic words XYZZY, PLUGH and PLOVER. All
    three are tags.
  - "No longer unbearable": drop the BEAR.
  - "Free bird… do it twice": FREE BIRD.
  - "When rust is a problem": OIL.

  The original 1970s game read only the first 5 letters of each word. Its
  commands were tags of five letters at most, so the game's whole vocabulary was
  a shorter tag pattern than ours.

● The prediction holds: the game reads exactly the first five letters of each
  word.

  ┌─────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │        You type         │             The game reads it as              │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ xyzzy                   │ XYZZY (teleports: ">>Foof!<<")                │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ xyzzyfoo                │ XYZZY: the "foo" is ignored, and it teleports │
  │                         │  back                                         │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ xyzz                    │ unknown: four letters aren't enough           │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ inventory, invenxyz,    │ INVEN: all three list the inventory           │
  │ inven                   │                                               │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ inv                     │ unknown                                       │
  └─────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  So a word is its first five letters, and anything after that is never read.
  That's the PDP-10's 36-bit machine word: five 7-bit characters take 35 bits.
  Open-adventure kept that rule from the original FORTRAN even though modern
  machines don't need it.

  There's a second match with your rule. In old-style mode, the game reports an
  unknown word in uppercase: you type inv and it answers "INV". The 1970s parser
  promoted every word lower → upper before matching: the subject became the
  object, then it was looked up as a tag of five letters at most. Your
  /A-Z0-9/{1,8} is the same design, sized to the machine word of today: 8 × 7 =
  56 bits, inside 64.

❯ so the game is a proof tree

● In the sense your work uses it, yes, and the structure is concrete enough to
  check:
  - Each location is a state.
  - Each command is a tag literal: five letters, promoted to uppercase.
  - The travel rules are the inference rules: "from this room, this word leads
    to that room, if this holds".
  - A playthrough is a derivation, and the 430 points are the whole proof.

❯ we can reverse enginer with agents to validate

● Yes: map the dungeon by exploring it rather than reading its source. Each move
  is replayed from the same seed, so every finding is reproducible.
  - Start: each agent starts from the seed, with the lamp lit.
  - Explore: from each room it has reached, it tries each movement word (N, S,
    E, W, the diagonals, U, D, IN, OUT, and the magic words XYZZY, PLUGH and
    PLOVER), then look to see where it landed.
  - Record: every move that works becomes a triple: (room, WORD, room).
  - Validate: a found route is checked by replaying it from the seed and
    confirming it reaches the same room.

  That gives the travel table from behaviour alone, written as the S-P-O triples
  the rest of the work uses:
